In the drowsy heart of a humid August afternoon, light falls through a yellowed screen door onto the kitchen of a borrowed lake cottage, where a twenty-eight-year-old adult Chinese woman stands with her back to the threshold. She has the compact build and rounded shoulders of someone who swims regularly in colder months, her black hair pinned in a loose, damp bun that is beginning to unravel at the nape. A thin cotton sundress, faded to the color of old parchment, clings to her shoulder blades and the small of her back where sweat has darkened the fabric in irregular shapes. The dress is meant to button at the front, but three upper fastenings have been left open, the edges parting to show the inner curve of her breasts unsupported beneath, the material pulling taut against her nipples in the humid stillness. Her left hand grips the handle of the refrigerator, door cracked to release a small breath of cool air across her bare calves; her right hand hangs at her side with the fingers slightly curled, as if she has just released something. She is looking back over her shoulder toward the screen, her lower lip held between her teeth, her gaze directed not at anything in the room but at the gap between two pines where a neighbor's dock is partially visible through the trees, the question of whether anyone stands there remaining unanswered and therefore present. The kitchen behind her is bare, a single ceramic mug on the counter, a dish towel faded to gray loops. The camera holds her at medium distance from a position just inside the doorway, the screen mesh softening the forest green behind her into a mottled haze, the focus sharp on the sweat-darkened cotton and the visible tension in her forearm as she maintains her grip on the refrigerator.
